<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> We prove that not every hyperbolicity cone is a spectrahedral shadow: some closed hyperbolicity cones admit no finite affine semidefinite lift, even with arbitrary real coefficients and any finite number of auxiliary variables. This disproves the Projected Lax Conjecture and hence the generalized Lax conjecture.

- **claim-main-no-lift** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-main)): There exists a real homogeneous hyperbolic polynomial whose closed hyperbolicity cone has no finite semidefinite lift.
  - Explanation: Some closed hyperbolicity cone is not a spectrahedral shadow, so no finite semidefinite lift exists even with arbitrary real coefficients and any number of auxiliary variables.
- **claim-hyperbolic-block-cone** (theorem, [source](#stmt-prop-hyperbolic)): The polynomial $p_Q$ is homogeneous of degree $n(n+1)$ and hyperbolic with respect to $e=(I_n,I_n,0)$, with $p_Q(e)=1$. Fix $y$ and choose vectors $b_1,\ldots,b_n\in\mathbb R^r$ with $Q(y)=\sum_{j=1}^n b_jb_j^\top$. Define $$B_j=\begin{pmatrix}0&b_j^\top\\-b_j&0\end{pmatrix}, \qquad \mathcal B=\begin{pmatrix}B_1\\\vdots\\B_n\end{pmatrix}.$$ Then the closed hyperbolicity cone $K_Q$ of $p_Q$ is characterized, at this value of $y$, by $$\label{eq:block-cone} (X,Z,y)\in K_Q \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad \begin{pmatrix}I_n\otimes X&\mathcal B\\ \mathcal B^\top&Z\end{pmatrix}\succeq0.$$
  - Explanation: For a positive semidefinite quadratic matrix map the determinantal polynomial is hyperbolic in a fixed direction, and at each fixed input its closed cone is a block positive semidefinite condition from a Gram factorization.
- **claim-epigraph-section** (theorem, [source](#stmt-prop-epigraph)): The set $E_Q$ is the image of the affine section $K_Q\cap\{X=\mathop{\mathrm{diag}}(1,A):A\in\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}_r\}$ under the projection $(X,Z,y)\mapsto(A,Z_{00},y)$. Consequently, if $K_Q$ is a spectrahedral shadow, then so is $E_Q$.
  - Explanation: The epigraph-type set defined with the Moore–Penrose inverse is exactly a coordinate projection of an affine section of the cone, so a lift of the cone would give a lift of that set.
- **claim-seed-matrix** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-seed)): There is a positive semidefinite matrix $T\in\mathcal H$ of rank $d_*=20$ with the following properties. Its restriction to $L$ is positive definite. If $$J=\ker T,\qquad K=\operatorname{proj}_P J, \qquad H_0=\{p\in H:T(p,L)=0\},$$ then $\dim K=k=16$, multiplication $$\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}^2K\longrightarrow\mathbb R[x_1,\ldots,x_7]_4$$ is injective, and $$\label{eq:pure-cubic-spanning} K\langle x_1,\ldots,x_7\rangle =\mathbb R[x_1,\ldots,x_7]_3.$$ Finally, $JH_0$ has codimension $9$ in $\mathbb R[x]_4$.
  - Explanation: There is a positive semidefinite Hankel seed matrix of the stated rank, positive definite on the linear part, whose kernel has injective pairwise products and spans the pure cubics after multiplication by linear forms.
- **claim-rank-stratum-manifold** (classification, [source](#stmt-lem-hankel-stratum)): The set $\mathcal X$ is a smooth semialgebraic manifold of dimension $$D=330-1-\frac{k(k+1)}2=193.$$ Before projectivization, the tangent space at a representative $Y$ is the space of $Y'\in\mathcal H$ whose restriction to $\ker Y\times\ker Y$ is zero.
  - Explanation: Projective Hankel classes with the required rank, kernel and multiplication conditions form a smooth semialgebraic manifold of explicit dimension with a described tangent space.
- **claim-kernel-affine-independence** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-kernel-affine-action)): For every $[Y]\in\mathcal X$, the map $$\Delta_{K_Y}:\mathbb R^7\longrightarrow \operatorname{Hom}(K_Y,\mathbb R[s]_1), \qquad t\longmapsto(p\mapsto\partial_t p)$$ is injective, and the coefficient maps $a,b$ in <a href="#eq:kernel-graph" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:kernel-graph">[eq:kernel-graph]</a> are not both zero. The seven infinitesimal translations and the infinitesimal dilation give eight independent variations of the kernel graph. These assertions, including the independence of the variations, also hold over $\mathbf C$ under the same rank and multiplication hypotheses.
  - Explanation: Translations act injectively on the kernel graph and together with dilation give independent variations, so these symmetries can be divided out, also over the complex numbers.
- **claim-hankel-normalization** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-hankel-normalization)): The normalized matrices, together with their data $(K_Y,b,a)$, form a semialgebraic set $\mathcal M$ of dimension $185$. Normalization is continuous, and each $[Y]\in\mathcal X$ is uniquely recovered from its normalized data and its seven translation parameters and positive dilation parameter. Moreover, $\mathcal M$ has a compact ambient semialgebraic space in which the projection to $\mathop{\mathrm{Gr}}(k,P)$ is continuous.
  - Explanation: Explicit normalizations of translation and dilation give a semialgebraic set of normalized data of reduced dimension from which each class is uniquely recovered.
- **claim-finite-normalized-fiber** (construction, [source](#stmt-prop-finite-normalized-fiber)): The matrix $T$ in Lemma <a href="#lem:seed" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:seed">4</a> may be chosen so that, for $K=\operatorname{proj}_P\ker T$,* 1. *the fiber $\mathcal M_K$ of $\mathcal M\to\mathop{\mathrm{Gr}}(k,P)$ is finite;* 2. *no point of $\overline{\mathcal M}\setminus\mathcal M$ projects to $K$.
  - Explanation: By fiber and frontier dimension counts the seed can be chosen with finite normalized fiber and no boundary point projecting to its kernel space.
- **claim-uniform-rank-correction** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-hankel-rank-correction)): Use the fixed decomposition $H=W\oplus K$ from <a href="#eq:fixed-complements" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:fixed-complements">[eq:fixed-complements]</a>. Suppose $Y_i\in\mathcal H$ have blocks $$Y_i=\begin{pmatrix}A_i&B_i\\B_i^T&D_i\end{pmatrix},$$ where $A_i$ is invertible, $\sup_i\\|A_i^{-1}\\|<\infty$, and $$A_i^{-1}B_i\longrightarrow0, \qquad D_i-B_i^TA_i^{-1}B_i\longrightarrow0.$$ There are Hankel perturbations $\Delta_i\to0$ such that $Y_i^\sharp=Y_i+\Delta_i$ has rank $d_*$. Its $W$ compression $A_i^\sharp$ satisfies $$(A_i^\sharp)^{-1}-A_i^{-1}\longrightarrow0,$$ and $\ker Y_i^\sharp\to K$, or equivalently $\mathop{\mathrm{ran}}Y_i^\sharp\to W$.
  - Explanation: Under uniform invertibility and vanishing Schur data, small Hankel perturbations give exact low rank with nearby inverse compressions and converging kernels.
- **claim-finite-inverse-limits** (classification, [source](#stmt-lem-inverse-limits)): Let $T,K,R,W$, and $\mathcal C$ be chosen as above. Suppose a sequence $Y_i\in\mathcal H$ has a group of $d_*$ eigenvalues bounded away from zero in absolute value, and the span of the corresponding eigenvectors tends to $W$. Suppose all remaining eigenvalues tend to zero. If the inverses of the compressions $A_i=(Y_i)_{W,W}$ converge to a nonzero matrix $C$, then $$[C]\in\mathcal C.$$
  - Explanation: Any nonzero limit of inverse compressions along sequences with the stated spectral behavior has its projective class in the finite fixed set.
- **claim-generic-vector-subspace** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-generic-wu)): There exist a unit vector $w=(w_1,\ldots,w_N)\in W^N$ and an $r$-dimensional subspace $U\subset w^\perp\subset H^N$ with the following properties.* 1. *The vectors $w_1,\ldots,w_N$ span $W$, and $$\label{eq:inverse-pairings} \sum_{i=1}^N w_i^T Cw_i\ne0 \qquad\text{whenever }[C]\in\mathcal C.$$* 2. *The map $$\label{eq:projection-U} P_W^N\big\|_U:U\longrightarrow w^\perp\cap W^N$$ is an isomorphism.* 3. *Suppose that $F_\infty,F_0\subset H$ are orthogonal subspaces, $C_0$ is a symmetric operator on $F_0$, and $\dim(F_\infty\oplus F_0)>d_*$. No nonzero tuple $u=(u_1,\ldots,u_N)\in U$ satisfies $$\label{eq:forbidden-projections} P_{F_\infty}u_i=0,\qquad P_{F_0}u_i=C_0P_{F_0}w_i \qquad(1\le i\le N).$$
  - Explanation: A generic unit vector and subspace avoid the finitely many vanishing pairings and all forbidden projection patterns by a Grassmannian dimension count.
- **claim-separation-from-closure** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-separation)): For $w$ and $U$ as in Lemma <a href="#lem:generic-wu" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:generic-wu">11</a>, let $$\mathcal S_U= \{(I_N\otimes Y)u: u\in U,\ Y\text{ a Hankel matrix on }H\}.$$ Then $w\notin\overline{\mathcal S_U}$.
  - Explanation: The chosen vector lies outside the closure of all componentwise Hankel images of the subspace, including limits from unbounded matrices.
- **claim-polynomial-patch** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-polynomial-patch)): There is an open ball $\Omega_0\subset\mathbb R^7$ about zero such that $M(s)$ has rank $N$ for every $s\in\Omega_0$. The polynomial map $$\label{eq:polynomial-patch} x(s)=\bigl(M(s)M(s)^T,\ \mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(gG(y_s)),\ y_s\bigr)$$ has degree at most four and takes $\Omega_0$ into $E_Q$. Its values satisfy the trace inequality defining $E_Q$ with equality.
  - Explanation: Evaluation functionals give a low-degree polynomial patch of full-rank data inside the epigraph set, meeting its trace inequality with equality.
- **claim-moment-violation** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-moment-violation)): There exist a linear functional $\Lambda:\mathbb R[s_1,\ldots,s_7]_{\le4}\to\mathbb R$ and an open ball $\Omega\subset\Omega_0$ about zero with the following properties:* 1. *$\Lambda(1)=1$, and its moment matrix on polynomials of degree at most two is positive definite;* 2. *for every $c\in\Omega$, writing $$\label{eq:tested-patch} (A_{c,\varepsilon},t_{c,\varepsilon},y_{c,\varepsilon}) =\Lambda_s\bigl(x(c+\varepsilon s)\bigr),$$ there are constants $a_c>0$ and $\kappa_c>0$ such that, as $\varepsilon\downarrow0$, $$\begin{gathered} \label{eq:deformation-lower-bound} A_{c,\varepsilon}\succeq a_c\varepsilon^4 I_r,\\ \label{eq:deformation-violation} t_{c,\varepsilon}-\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\bigl(A_{c,\varepsilon}^{-1}Q(y_{c,\varepsilon})\bigr) =-\kappa_c\varepsilon^4+o(\varepsilon^4). \end{gathered}$$ All three entries of <a href="#eq:tested-patch" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tested-patch">[eq:tested-patch]</a> remain bounded as $\varepsilon\downarrow0$.
  - Explanation: A positive definite moment functional at small scales yields points violating the trace inequality at fourth order while their matrix coordinates stay bounded below at the same order.
- **claim-positive-moment-extension** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-positive-moment-extension)): Let $n\ge1$ and $j\ge0$ be integers, let $z=(z_1,\ldots,z_n)$, and let the linear functional $\lambda:\mathbb R[z]_{\le2j}\to\mathbb R$ satisfy $\lambda(p^2)>0$ for every nonzero $p\in\mathbb R[z]_{\le j}$. For every integer $b\ge j$, there is an extension $\widetilde\lambda:\mathbb R[z]_{\le2b}\to\mathbb R$ such that $\widetilde\lambda(p^2)>0$ for every nonzero $p\in\mathbb R[z]_{\le b}$.
  - Explanation: A strictly positive truncated functional extends to any higher truncation degree while staying strictly positive on nonzero squares.
- **claim-analytic-gram-factors** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-analytic-lift)): Let $C\subset\mathbb R^a$ have an exact representation $$C=\{x\in\mathbb R^a:\text{there exists }u\in\mathbb R^\nu \text{ with }\mathcal L(x,u)\succeq0\},$$ where $\nu\ge0$ and $\mathcal L$ is an affine symmetric pencil of size $\ell\ge1$. Let $\Omega\subset\mathbb R^n$ be nonempty, open, and semialgebraic, and let $p:\mathbb R^n\to\mathbb R^a$ be polynomial with $p(\Omega)\subset C$. There is a nonempty open subset $\Omega'\subset\Omega$ and real analytic maps $u:\Omega'\to\mathbb R^\nu$ and $F:\Omega'\to\mathbb R^{\ell\times\ell}$ such that $$\label{eq:analytic-Gram-identity} \mathcal L(p(s),u(s))=F(s)F(s)^T\qquad(s\in\Omega').$$
  - Explanation: If a set has an exact semidefinite lift, a polynomial patch inside it admits analytic lift variables and analytic Gram factors on a nonempty open part.
- **claim-moment-near-feasibility** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-moment-near-feasibility)): Let $C$, $p$, and $\Omega$ be as in Lemma <a href="#lem:analytic-lift" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:analytic-lift">16</a>. Fix an integer $b\ge\max\{1,\deg p\}$ and a linear functional $\lambda:\mathbb R[z_1,\ldots,z_n]_{\le2b}\to\mathbb R$ satisfying $\lambda(1)=1$ and $\lambda(q^2)\ge0$ for all $q\in\mathbb R[z]_{\le b}$. There is a nonempty open subset $\Omega'\subset\Omega$ such that, for each $c\in\Omega'$, the points $$x_\varepsilon=\lambda_z\bigl(p(c+\varepsilon z)\bigr)$$ admit points $\widetilde x_\varepsilon\in C$, for all sufficiently small $\varepsilon>0$, with $$\label{eq:near-feasibility} \\|\widetilde x_\varepsilon-x_\varepsilon\\|=O(\varepsilon^b).$$ The implied constant may depend on $c$ and on the displayed data.
  - Explanation: Under a lift, a finite positive moment functional applied to a polynomial patch gives points within a controlled order of exactly feasible points.
- **claim-no-lift-epigraph-cone** (theorem, [source](#stmt-prop-no-lift)): For the admissible quadratic matrix map $Q$ of <a href="#eq:constructed-Q" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:constructed-Q">[eq:constructed-Q]</a>, the set $E_Q$ is not a spectrahedral shadow. Consequently its associated entire closed hyperbolicity cone $K_Q$ is not a spectrahedral shadow.
  - Explanation: For the constructed admissible map, the fourth-order violation contradicts the higher-order feasible approximation a finite lift would force, so neither the epigraph set nor the cone is a spectrahedral shadow.

        "anchor": "stmt-prop-hyperbolic",
        "exact_quote": "The polynomial $p_Q$ is homogeneous of degree $n(n+1)$ and hyperbolic with respect to $e=(I_n,I_n,0)$, with $p_Q(e)=1$. Fix $y$ and choose vectors $b_1,\\ldots,b_n\\in\\mathbb R^r$ with $Q(y)=\\sum_{j=1}^n b_jb_j^\\top$. Define $$B_j=\\begin{pmatrix}0&b_j^\\top\\\\-b_j&0\\end{pmatrix},\n \\qquad\n \\mathcal B=\\begin{pmatrix}B_1\\\\\\vdots\\\\B_n\\end{pmatrix}.$$ Then the closed hyperbolicity cone $K_Q$ of $p_Q$ is characterized, at this value of $y$, by $$\\label{eq:block-cone}\n (X,Z,y)\\in K_Q\n \\quad\\Longleftrightarrow\\quad\n \\begin{pmatrix}I_n\\otimes X&\\mathcal B\\\\\n                 \\mathcal B^\\top&Z\\end{pmatrix}\\succeq0.$$",
        "source_id": "PM-095.1"
      },

        "anchor": "stmt-lem-seed",
        "exact_quote": "There is a positive semidefinite matrix $T\\in\\mathcal H$ of rank $d_*=20$ with the following properties. Its restriction to $L$ is positive definite.       },

        "exact_quote": "There exist a unit vector $w=(w_1,\\ldots,w_N)\\in W^N$ and an $r$-dimensional subspace $U\\subset w^\\perp\\subset H^N$ with the following properties.*\n\n\n1.  *The vectors $w_1,\\ldots,w_N$ span $W$, and $$\\label{eq:inverse-pairings}\n     \\sum_{i=1}^N w_i^T Cw_i\\ne0\n     \\qquad\\text{whenever }[C]\\in\\mathcal C.$$*\n\n\n2.  *The map $$\\label{eq:projection-U}\n     P_W^N\\big|_U:U\\longrightarrow w^\\perp\\cap W^N$$ is an isomorphism.*\n\n\n3.  *Suppose that $F_\\infty,F_0\\subset H$ are orthogonal subspaces, $C_0$ is a symmetric operator on $F_0$, and $\\dim(F_\\infty\\oplus F_0)>d_*$. No nonzero tuple $u=(u_1,\\ldots,u_N)\\in U$ satisfies $$\\label{eq:forbidden-projections}\n     P_{F_\\infty}u_i=0,\\qquad\n     P_{F_0}u_i=C_0P_{F_0}w_i\n     \\qquad(1\\le i\\le N).$$",
        "source_id": "PM-095.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-lem-generic-wu",

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-moment-violation",
        "exact_quote": "There exist a linear functional $\\Lambda:\\mathbb R[s_1,\\ldots,s_7]_{\\le4}\\to\\mathbb R$ and an open ball $\\Omega\\subset\\Omega_0$ about zero with the following properties:*\n\n1.  *$\\Lambda(1)=1$, and its moment matrix on polynomials of degree at most two is positive definite;*\n\n\n\n\n2.  *for every $c\\in\\Omega$, writing $$\\label{eq:tested-patch}\n      (A_{c,\\varepsilon},t_{c,\\varepsilon},y_{c,\\varepsilon})\n      =\\Lambda_s\\bigl(x(c+\\varepsilon s)\\bigr),$$ there are constants $a_c>0$ and $\\kappa_c>0$ such that, as $\\varepsilon\\downarrow0$, $$\\begin{gathered}\n      \\label{eq:deformation-lower-bound}\n      A_{c,\\varepsilon}\\succeq a_c\\varepsilon^4 I_r,\\\\\n      \\label{eq:deformation-violation}\n      t_{c,\\varepsilon}-\\mathop{\\mathrm{tr}}\\bigl(A_{c,\\varepsilon}^{-1}Q(y_{c,\\varepsilon})\\bigr)\n      =-\\kappa_c\\varepsilon^4+o(\\varepsilon^4).\n     \n    \\end{gathered}$$ All three entries of <a href=\"#eq:tested-patch\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:tested-patch\">[eq:tested-patch]</a> remain bounded as $\\varepsilon\\downarrow0$.",
        "source_id": "PM-095.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-lem-moment-violation",
          "exact_quote": "There exist a linear functional $\\Lambda:\\mathbb R[s_1,\\ldots,s_7]_{\\le4}\\to\\mathbb R$ and an open ball $\\Omega\\subset\\Omega_0$ about zero with the following properties:*\n\n1.  *$\\Lambda(1)=1$, and its moment matrix on polynomials of degree at most two is positive definite;*\n\n\n\n\n2.  *for every $c\\in\\Omega$, writing $$\\label{eq:tested-patch}\n      (A_{c,\\varepsilon},t_{c,\\varepsilon},y_{c,\\varepsilon})\n      =\\Lambda_s\\bigl(x(c+\\varepsilon s)\\bigr),$$ there are constants $a_c>0$ and $\\kappa_c>0$ such that, as $\\varepsilon\\downarrow0$, $$\\begin{gathered}\n      \\label{eq:deformation-lower-bound}\n      A_{c,\\varepsilon}\\succeq a_c\\varepsilon^4 I_r,\\\\\n      \\label{eq:deformation-violation}\n      t_{c,\\varepsilon}-\\mathop{\\mathrm{tr}}\\bigl(A_{c,\\varepsilon}^{-1}Q(y_{c,\\varepsilon})\\bigr)\n      =-\\kappa_c\\varepsilon^4+o(\\varepsilon^4).\n     \n    \\end{gathered}$$ All three entries of <a href=\"#eq:tested-patch\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:tested-patch\">[eq:tested-patch]</a> remain bounded as $\\varepsilon\\downarrow0$.",
          "source_id": "PM-095.1"
        }

        "exact_quote": "Let $n\\ge1$ and $j\\ge0$ be integers, let $z=(z_1,\\ldots,z_n)$, and let the linear functional $\\lambda:\\mathbb R[z]_{\\le2j}\\to\\mathbb R$ satisfy $\\lambda(p^2)>0$ for every nonzero $p\\in\\mathbb R[z]_{\\le j}$.     {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-analytic-lift",
        "exact_quote": "Let $C\\subset\\mathbb R^a$ have an exact representation $$C=\\{x\\in\\mathbb R^a:\\text{there exists }u\\in\\mathbb R^\\nu\n                         \\text{ with }\\mathcal L(x,u)\\succeq0\\},$$ where $\\nu\\ge0$ and $\\mathcal L$ is an affine symmetric pencil of size $\\ell\\ge1$. Let $\\Omega\\subset\\mathbb R^n$ be nonempty, open, and semialgebraic, and let $p:\\mathbb R^n\\to\\mathbb R^a$ be polynomial with $p(\\Omega)\\subset C$. There is a nonempty open subset $\\Omega'\\subset\\Omega$ and real analytic maps $u:\\Omega'\\to\\mathbb R^\\nu$ and $F:\\Omega'\\to\\mathbb R^{\\ell\\times\\ell}$ such that $$\\label{eq:analytic-Gram-identity}\n \\mathcal L(p(s),u(s))=F(s)F(s)^T\\qquad(s\\in\\Omega').$$",
        "source_id": "PM-095.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-lem-analytic-lift",
          "exact_quote": "Let $C\\subset\\mathbb R^a$ have an exact representation $$C=\\{x\\in\\mathbb R^a:\\text{there exists }u\\in\\mathbb R^\\nu\n                         \\text{ with }\\mathcal L(x,u)\\succeq0\\},$$ where $\\nu\\ge0$ and $\\mathcal L$ is an affine symmetric pencil of size $\\ell\\ge1$. Let $\\Omega\\subset\\mathbb R^n$ be nonempty, open, and semialgebraic, and let $p:\\mathbb R^n\\to\\mathbb R^a$ be polynomial with $p(\\Omega)\\subset C$. There is a nonempty open subset $\\Omega'\\subset\\Omega$ and real analytic maps $u:\\Omega'\\to\\mathbb R^\\nu$ and $F:\\Omega'\\to\\mathbb R^{\\ell\\times\\ell}$ such that $$\\label{eq:analytic-Gram-identity}\n \\mathcal L(p(s),u(s))=F(s)F(s)^T\\qquad(s\\in\\Omega').$$",
          "source_id": "PM-095.1"
        }

      "exact_statement": "Let $C\\subset\\mathbb R^a$ have an exact representation $$C=\\{x\\in\\mathbb R^a:\\text{there exists }u\\in\\mathbb R^\\nu\n                         \\text{ with }\\mathcal L(x,u)\\succeq0\\},$$ where $\\nu\\ge0$ and $\\mathcal L$ is an affine symmetric pencil of size $\\ell\\ge1$. Consequently its associated entire closed hyperbolicity cone $K_Q$ is not a spectrahedral shadow.",

<a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **hyperbolic polynomial** ([source](#pm-h-2)): A real homogeneous polynomial $p$ on a finite-dimensional real vector space is *hyperbolic with respect to* $e$ if $p(e)>0$ and, for every $x$, all roots of $t\mapsto p(te-x)$ are real.
- **spectrahedron** ([source](#eq:lift-definition)): A *spectrahedron* is a set defined by a finite affine linear matrix inequality.
- **spectrahedral shadow** ([source](#eq:lift-definition)): A *spectrahedral shadow*, or a set with a finite semidefinite lift, is a set $S\subset\mathbb R^d$ of the form $$\label{eq:lift-definition} S=\left\{x\in\mathbb R^d:\exists u\in\mathbb R^a, A_0+\sum_{i=1}^d x_iA_i+\sum_{j=1}^a u_jB_j\succeq0\right\},$$ where $a$ and the common size of the real symmetric matrices are finite.
- **Projected Lax Conjecture** ([source](#eq:lift-definition)): The weaker *Projected Lax Conjecture*, formulated by Netzer and Sanyal (Netzer and Sanyal 2015) [[NetzerSanyal2015](#ref-NetzerSanyal2015)], asks whether every hyperbolicity cone is a spectrahedral shadow.
- **epigraph set E_Q** ([source](#eq:EQ)): For a positive semidefinite matrix $A$, let $A^\dagger$ denote its Moore–Penrose inverse. Define $$\label{eq:EQ} E_Q=\left\{(A,t,y): \begin{array}{l} A\in\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}_r,\ A\succeq0,\quad \mathop{\mathrm{ran}}Q(y)\subseteq\mathop{\mathrm{ran}}A,\\[2pt] t\geq\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\bigl(A^\dagger Q(y)\bigr) \end{array}\right\}.$$
- **Hankel matrix space** ([source](#eq:hankel-functional)): Let $\mathcal H\subset\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}(H)$ be the space of Hankel matrices $$\label{eq:hankel-functional} Y(p,p')=y(pp'),\qquad p,p'\in H, \qquad y\in\mathbb R[x]_4^*.$$
- **complements R and W** ([source](#eq:fixed-complements)): Fix this $T$ and $K$ for the rest of the paper, and set $$\label{eq:fixed-complements} R=K^\perp\subset P,\qquad W=L\oplus R.$$
- **separating operator g** ([source](#eq:g-properties)): Define the symmetric operator $$g=\delta^{-2}P_{w^\perp}-P_{\mathbb Rw}.$$

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **s-hyperbolic** [claim](#stmt-prop-hyperbolic) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-hyperbolic): Gram factorization and determinant identity on each line -> hyperbolicity and block cone description
- **s-epigraph** [claim](#stmt-prop-epigraph) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-epigraph): Singular Schur complement criterion applied to the block cone -> E_Q as projection of affine section
- **s-seed** [claim](#stmt-lem-seed) / [proof](#app:certificate): Orbit moment matrix, modular minors, lifting, deformation along an arc -> real seed matrix
- **s-stratum** [claim](#stmt-lem-hankel-stratum) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-hankel-stratum): Schur complement and implicit function theorem -> smooth rank stratum
- **s-affine-action** [claim](#stmt-lem-kernel-affine-action) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-kernel-affine-action): Cubic spanning and rank argument -> independent affine variations
- **s-normalization** [claim](#stmt-lem-hankel-normalization) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-hankel-normalization): Explicit translation and dilation normalization -> normalized set and compact ambient space
- **s-finite-fiber** [claim](#stmt-prop-finite-normalized-fiber) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-finite-normalized-fiber): Fiber and frontier dimension counts -> seed with finite fiber
- **s-rank-correction** [claim](#stmt-lem-hankel-rank-correction) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-hankel-rank-correction): Uniform contraction mapping on the Schur complement -> rank-corrected sequences
- **s-inverse-limits** [claim](#stmt-lem-inverse-limits) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-inverse-limits): Compactness of normalized data and dilation computation -> finite set of inverse limits
- **s-generic-wu** [claim](#stmt-lem-generic-wu) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-generic-wu): Genericity and Grassmannian dimension count -> vector w and subspace U
- **s-separation** [claim](#stmt-lem-separation) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-separation): Spectral limits of Hankel images and contradiction with pairings -> w outside closure of Hankel images
- **s-admissible-Q** [claim](#eq:constructed-Q) / [proof](#eq:constructed-Q): Quadratic form g nonnegative on images -> admissible map Q
- **s-patch** [claim](#stmt-lem-polynomial-patch) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-polynomial-patch): Rank of M(0) and range inclusion -> patch inside E_Q
- **s-moment-violation** [claim](#stmt-lem-moment-violation) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-moment-violation): Rescaled residual and continuity of leading coefficient -> fourth-order violation
- **s-moment-extension** [claim](#stmt-lem-positive-moment-extension) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-positive-moment-extension): Schur complement with Gaussian top moments -> positive extension
- **s-analytic-lift** [claim](#stmt-lem-analytic-lift) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-analytic-lift): Stratification, Sard, analytic submersion -> analytic Gram factors
- **s-near-feasibility** [claim](#stmt-lem-moment-near-feasibility) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-moment-near-feasibility): Taylor polynomials, moment test and mixing with a maximal-rank feasible point -> nearby feasible points
- **s-no-lift** [claim](#stmt-prop-no-lift) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-no-lift): Contradiction between violation order and approximation order -> E_Q and K_Q not spectrahedral shadows
- **s-main** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-no-lift-2): Combine hyperbolicity with non-liftability -> main theorem

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **inv-block-determinant-identity**: At a fixed input y, once Q(y) is written as a sum of rank-one Gram terms, the value of the polynomial p_Q equals the determinant of the symmetric block matrix assembled from I_n tensor X, the stacked antisymmetric-bordered Gram blocks, and Z. This holds for every X, including singular X, because both sides are polynomial in X and Z.
- **inv-cone-block-characterization**: At a fixed y, membership of (X,Z,y) in the closed hyperbolicity cone of p_Q is equivalent to positive semidefiniteness of the block matrix built from the Gram factorization of Q(y); this is not claimed to be a joint linear matrix inequality in X, Z and y.
- **inv-no-lift-existential**: For the admissible quadratic matrix map constructed in the paper, the epigraph set E_Q is not a spectrahedral shadow. This is an existence result obtained from generic choices and a limiting contradiction; no finite procedure computes or checks it.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **anti-joint-lmi-reading**: Read the block matrix inequality as a linear matrix inequality jointly in X, Z and y and hand it to a semidefinite solver as a representation of the cone. -> The Gram factors depend on y, so the block matrix is not affine in y; the equivalence only holds one value of y at a time.
- **anti-approximate-lift-claim**: Cite the non-liftability theorem to rule out approximate or closure-based semidefinite representations. -> The theorem concerns exact finite lifts only; approximations or closures are outside what is proved.
- **anti-small-test-as-proof**: Treat a passing small-size check of the determinant identity as evidence for the high-dimensional non-liftability result. -> The identity check says nothing about lifts; the obstruction is a high-dimensional existence argument.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `algorithmic`. Only the finite determinant identity of the hyperbolicity proposition is executable: for a fixed y and a Gram factorization of Q(y), the adjugate formula for p_Q is evaluated and compared with the block matrix determinant. The main non-liftability theorem is existential and is not checked here.

```python
# INPUTS: whitespace-separated integers on stdin: r, then n=r+1 Gram vectors b_j (r integers each), then X (n*n row-major), then Z (n*n row-major).
# PRECONDITIONS: r>=1; X and Z symmetric; Q(y) is the sum of b_j b_j^T, hence positive semidefinite.
# OUTPUTS: one integer, the value det((det X) Z - Phi(adj X)) with Phi built from Q(y).
# TIME: cofactor expansion, factorial in n; intended for very small n.
# SPACE: O(n^2) per recursion level.
# ARITHMETIC: exact Python integers, no floating point.
# FAILURE: malformed or short input raises an exception and exits nonzero.
import sys

def det(M):
    n = len(M)
    if n == 0:
        return 1
    if n == 1:
        return M[0][0]
    s = 0
    for j in range(n):
        minor = [row[:j] + row[j + 1:] for row in M[1:]]
        s += (-1) ** j * M[0][j] * det(minor)
    return s

def adj(X):
    n = len(X)
    if n == 1:
        return [[1]]
    A = [[0] * n for _ in range(n)]
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(n):
            minor = [row[:j] + row[j + 1:] for k, row in enumerate(X) if k != i]
            A[j][i] = (-1) ** (i + j) * det(minor)
    return A

def main():
    tok = [int(t) for t in sys.stdin.read().split()]
    r = tok[0]
    n = r + 1
    pos = 1
    bs = []
    for _ in range(n):
        bs.append(tok[pos:pos + r])
        pos += r
    X = [tok[pos + i * n: pos + (i + 1) * n] for i in range(n)]
    pos += n * n
    Z = [tok[pos + i * n: pos + (i + 1) * n] for i in range(n)]
    Q = [[0] * r for _ in range(r)]
    for b in bs:
        for i in range(r):
            for j in range(r):
                Q[i][j] += b[i] * b[j]
    A = adj(X)
    a = A[0][0]
    v = [A[0][1 + i] for i in range(r)]
    T = [row[1:] for row in A[1:]]
    tr = sum(Q[i][j] * T[j][i] for i in range(r) for j in range(r))
    P = [[0] * n for _ in range(n)]
    P[0][0] = tr
    for j in range(r):
        P[0][1 + j] = -sum(v[i] * Q[i][j] for i in range(r))
        P[1 + j][0] = -sum(Q[j][i] * v[i] for i in range(r))
        for i in range(r):
            P[1 + i][1 + j] = a * Q[i][j]
    dX = det(X)
    M = [[dX * Z[i][j] - P[i][j] for j in range(n)] for i in range(n)]
    print(det(M))

main()
```

    "oracle_code": "import sys\nfrom fractions import Fraction\n\ndef gdet(M):\n    n = len(M)\n    A = [[Fraction(x) for x in row] for row in M]\n    d = Fraction(1)\n    for c in range(n):\n        p = None\n        for q in range(c, n):\n            if A[q][c] != 0:\n                p = q\n                break\n        if p is None:\n            return Fraction(0)\n        if p != c:\n            A[c], A[p] = A[p], A[c]\n            d = -d\n        d *= A[c][c]\n        for q in range(c + 1, n):\n            f = A[q][c] / A[c][c]\n            for k in range(c, n):\n                A[q][k] -= f * A[c][k]\n    return d\n\ndef main():\n    try:\n        tok = [int(t) for t in open(sys.argv[1]).read().split()]\n        out = open(sys.argv[2]).read().split()\n        exp = sys.argv[3].strip()\n    except Exception:\n        sys.exit(1)\n    r = tok[0]\n    n = r + 1\n    pos = 1\n    bs = []\n    for _ in range(n):\n        bs.append(tok[pos:pos + r])\n        pos += r\n    X = [tok[pos + i * n: pos + (i + 1) * n] for i in range(n)]\n    pos += n * n\n    Z = [tok[pos + i * n: pos + (i + 1) * n] for i in range(n)]\n    N = n * n + n\n    D = [[0] * N for _ in range(N)]\n    for blk in range(n):\n        for i in range(n):\n            for j in range(n):\n                D[blk * n + i][blk * n + j] = X[i][j]\n    for j, b in enumerate(bs):\n        for i in range(n):\n            for k in range(n):\n                if i == 0 and k >= 1:\n                    val = b[k - 1]\n                elif k == 0 and i >= 1:\n                    val = -b[i - 1]\n                else:\n                    val = 0\n                D[j * n + i][n * n + k] = val\n                D[n * n + k][j * n + i] = val\n    for i in range(n):\n        for k in range(n):\n            D[n * n + i][n * n + k] = Z[i][k]\n    dv = gdet(D)\n    if dv.denominator != 1:\n        sys.exit(1)\n    dv = int(dv)\n    if len(out) != 1:\n        sys.exit(1)\n    try:\n        got = int(out[0])\n    except Exception:\n        sys.exit(1)\n    if got != dv:\n        sys.exit(1)\n    if exp != 'AGREE':\n        try:\n            if int(exp) != dv:\n                sys.exit(1)\n        except Exception:\n            sys.exit(1)\n    sys.exit(0)\n\nmain()\n",
    "output_contract": "Single integer line equal to p_Q(X,Z,y) at the fixed y defined by the Gram vectors.",
    "reason": "Only the finite determinant identity of the hyperbolicity proposition is executable: for a fixed y and a Gram factorization of Q(y), the adjugate formula for p_Q is evaluated and compared with the block matrix determinant. The main non-liftability theorem is existential and is not checked here.",
    "reference_code": "# INPUTS: whitespace-separated integers on stdin: r, then n=r+1 Gram vectors b_j (r integers each), then X (n*n row-major), then Z (n*n row-major).\n# PRECONDITIONS: r>=1; X and Z symmetric; Q(y) is the sum of b_j b_j^T, hence positive semidefinite.\n# OUTPUTS: one integer, the value det((det X) Z - Phi(adj X)) with Phi built from Q(y).\n# TIME: cofactor expansion, factorial in n; intended for very small n.\n# SPACE: O(n^2) per recursion level.\n# ARITHMETIC: exact Python integers, no floating point.\n# FAILURE: malformed or short input raises an exception and exits nonzero.\nimport sys\n\ndef det(M):\n    n = len(M)\n    if n == 0:\n        return 1\n    if n == 1:\n        return M[0][0]\n    s = 0\n    for j in range(n):\n        minor = [row[:j] + row[j + 1:] for row in M[1:]]\n        s += (-1) ** j * M[0][j] * det(minor)\n    return s\n\ndef adj(X):\n    n = len(X)\n    if n == 1:\n        return [[1]]\n    A = [[0] * n for _ in range(n)]\n    for i in range(n):\n        for j in range(n):\n            minor = [row[:j] + row[j + 1:] for k, row in enumerate(X) if k != i]\n            A[j][i] = (-1) ** (i + j) * det(minor)\n    return A\n\ndef main():\n    tok = [int(t) for t in sys.stdin.read().split()]\n    r = tok[0]\n    n = r + 1\n    pos = 1\n    bs = []\n    for _ in range(n):\n        bs.append(tok[pos:pos + r])\n        pos += r\n    X = [tok[pos + i * n: pos + (i + 1) * n] for i in range(n)]\n    pos += n * n\n    Z = [tok[pos + i * n: pos + (i + 1) * n] for i in range(n)]\n    Q = [[0] * r for _ in range(r)]\n    for b in bs:\n        for i in range(r):\n            for j in range(r):\n                Q[i][j] += b[i] * b[j]\n    A = adj(X)\n    a = A[0][0]\n    v = [A[0][1 + i] for i in range(r)]\n    T = [row[1:] for row in A[1:]]\n    tr = sum(Q[i][j] * T[j][i] for i in range(r) for j in range(r))\n    P = [[0] * n for _ in range(n)]\n    P[0][0] = tr\n    for j in range(r):\n        P[0][1 + j] = -sum(v[i] * Q[i][j] for i in range(r))\n        P[1 + j][0] = -sum(Q[j][i] * v[i] for i in range(r))\n        for i in range(r):\n            P[1 + i][1 + j] = a * Q[i][j]\n    dX = det(X)\n    M = [[dX * Z[i][j] - P[i][j] for j in range(n)] for i in range(n)]\n    print(det(M))\n\nmain()\n",

## 5. Domain engineering contracts

| domain | applies | strength | maturity | basis | why |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| software | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The result is an existence statement about a high-dimensional hyperbolic polynomial. It gives no procedure, interface or data format that a program could implement, and the paper's own appendix certificate is a one-off check, not a reusable routine. |
| games_simulation | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Nothing in the paper models a dynamical system, agent or physical process; it concerns convex-geometric representability of cones, so there is no simulation content to transfer. |
| tools | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper supplies no tool, solver or checker as a deliverable. It only establishes that no universal exact semidefinite lifting tool can exist for general hyperbolicity cones, which is a negative boundary for tool design. |
| algorithms | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The argument is a non-constructive contradiction: the choice of the vector, subspace and seed matrix relies on dimension counting and genericity, not on a finite procedure with a stated cost. No algorithm or complexity bound is claimed. |
| data_structures | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No representation, index or container is introduced. Hankel matrices and Gram factors are proof devices inside an impossibility argument, not storage schemes. |
| hardware_accelerators | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | There is no dataflow, arithmetic kernel or throughput claim. The finite-field rank certificate is a verification of an algebraic identity, not a workload for a device. |
| crypto_security | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No hardness assumption, protocol or adversary appears. The non-existence of finite lifts is a statement about convex sets, not about computational difficulty or secrecy. |
| machine_learning | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper concerns exact representability of cones and says nothing about learning, generalisation or training objectives, so it offers no transferable learning guarantee. |
| optimisation_operations_research | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The result bounds what semidefinite programming can model exactly: some hyperbolicity cones are not spectrahedral shadows, so convex programs over them cannot all be recast as exact semidefinite programs. It points toward native barrier methods, but supplies no solver or rate. |
| distributed_systems | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No communication, consensus or concurrency structure appears anywhere in the paper. |
| other | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The natural home of the result is real algebraic geometry and convex analysis (hyperbolic polynomials, spectrahedral shadows), which is mathematical rather than an engineering domain. It does not use the factorization theorem for cone lifts. |

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **limit-exact-lift-only** (formal_scope): The non-liftability result concerns exact finite affine semidefinite lifts only: the lifted set is the exact projection of a spectrahedron, and no closure of the projection is taken. Blocked use: ['Citing the theorem to rule out approximate, asymptotic or closure-based semidefinite representations', 'Using it to dismiss convergent relaxation hierarchies for hyperbolic constraints']
- **limit-existence-not-size-bound** (nonconstructive): The result is an existence argument in high dimension. It does not give a lower bound on the size of any particular representation, and it identifies no explicit small instance. Blocked use: ['Reading the theorem as a quantitative lower bound on lift size', 'Extrapolating non-liftability to low-dimensional or smooth-boundary cones, for which positive representation results exist']
- **limit-admissible-psd-map** (hypothesis): The determinantal polynomial is shown hyperbolic only for a matrix-valued quadratic map with homogeneous quadratic entries that is positive semidefinite at every real input. Without that global positivity the hyperbolicity and block-cone characterization are not available. Blocked use: ['Applying the determinant construction to a quadratic map that is indefinite at some input', 'Treating the cone description as valid outside the admissible class']
- **limit-block-cone-pointwise** (edge_case): The block matrix inequality describing the closed cone holds at a fixed value of the input y, with a Gram factorization chosen for that value. It is not asserted to be a linear matrix inequality jointly in all the variables. Blocked use: ['Passing the block inequality to a semidefinite solver as a global linear matrix inequality for the cone', 'Using it as a lift of the cone']
- **limit-large-N-regime** (hypothesis): The separation construction requires the repetition parameter N to exceed a threshold quadratic in the dimension of the quadratic-form space; the matrix size of the map is tied to N. The dimension count for the generic choice fails below the threshold. Blocked use: ['Instantiating the construction with a small N and expecting the separating vector and subspace to exist', 'Reading the matrix size as independent of N']
- **limit-generic-choice-nonconstructive** (nonconstructive): The vector and subspace are chosen generically, outside exceptional sets controlled only by dimension counts. No explicit vector or subspace is exhibited, so the admissible map is not given by a computable formula. Blocked use: ['Presenting the constructed quadratic map as an explicit, reproducible object', 'Deriving numerical or complexity guarantees from the generic choice']
- **limit-seed-certificate-chain** (formal_scope): The seed matrix rests on rank computations modulo a prime that are then lifted to characteristic zero and to a real positive semidefinite matrix by a genericity argument. The accompanying program is only an independent reproduction, not part of the proof. Blocked use: ['Treating a passing run of the modular check as a proof of the seed lemma', 'Replacing the characteristic-zero lifting argument by the finite-field computation alone']
- **limit-no-representing-measure** (counterexample_scope): The moment functionals and Hankel matrices are used purely algebraically. No representing measure is assumed, so probabilistic interpretations of these functionals are not licensed by the paper. Blocked use: ['Interpreting the Hankel matrices as moment matrices of probability measures', 'Importing measure-theoretic moment-problem results into the argument']

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We prove that not every hyperbolicity cone is a spectrahedral shadow: some closed hyperbolicity cones admit no finite affine semidefinite lift, even with arbitrary real coefficients and any finite number of auxiliary variables. This disproves the Projected Lax Conjecture and hence the generalized Lax conjecture.

<a id="pm-h-1"></a>

<a id="pm-h-2"></a>
### Introduction

A real homogeneous polynomial $p$ on a finite-dimensional real vector space is *hyperbolic with respect to* $e$ if $p(e)>0$ and, for every $x$, all roots of $t\mapsto p(te-x)$ are real. Its closed hyperbolicity cone is $$K(p,e)=\{x:\text{every root of }t\mapsto p(te-x)
                         \text{ is nonnegative}\}.$$ Gårding proved that this is a convex cone (Gårding 1959) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[Garding1959](#ref-Garding1959)]. The determinant on real symmetric matrices, with direction the identity, gives the positive semidefinite cone as a basic example. Hyperbolicity cones also support logarithmic self-concordant barriers, as shown by Güler (Güler 1997) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[Guler1997](#ref-Guler1997)], and hence form a natural setting for convex optimization beyond semidefinite programming.

<a id="eq:lift-definition"></a>
A *spectrahedron* is a set defined by a finite affine linear matrix inequality. A *spectrahedral shadow*, or a set with a finite semidefinite lift, is a set $S\subset\mathbb R^d$ of the form $$\label{eq:lift-definition}
 S=\left\{x\in\mathbb R^d:\exists u\in\mathbb R^a,
 A_0+\sum_{i=1}^d x_iA_i+\sum_{j=1}^a u_jB_j\succeq0\right\},$$ where $a$ and the common size of the real symmetric matrices are finite. Equality in this definition is exact; taking the closure of the displayed projection is not part of the definition. The *generalized Lax conjecture* asserts that every hyperbolicity cone is a spectrahedron. The weaker *Projected Lax Conjecture*, formulated by Netzer and Sanyal (Netzer and Sanyal 2015) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[NetzerSanyal2015](#ref-NetzerSanyal2015)], asks whether every hyperbolicity cone is a spectrahedral shadow.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1**. *There exists a real homogeneous hyperbolic polynomial whose closed hyperbolicity cone has no finite semidefinite lift.*

</div>

Thus both conjectures have a negative resolution. The assertion excludes every finite choice in <a href="#eq:lift-definition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lift-definition">[eq:lift-definition]</a>, with arbitrary real coefficients and any number of auxiliary variables. The construction is an existence argument in high dimension, not a lower bound on the size of a particular representation.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### Context and earlier obstructions

The original Lax question concerned homogeneous polynomials in three variables (Lax 1958) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[Lax1958](#ref-Lax1958)]. The determinantal theorem of Helton and Vinnikov (Helton and Vinnikov 2007) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[HeltonVinnikov2007](#ref-HeltonVinnikov2007)], together with the equivalence explained by Lewis, Parrilo, and Ramana (Lewis, Parrilo, and Ramana 2005) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[LewisParriloRamana2005](#ref-LewisParriloRamana2005)], gave an affirmative answer: a ternary hyperbolic polynomial has a definite symmetric determinantal representation. The distinction between a representation of a polynomial and a representation of its cone becomes essential in higher dimension. Brändén (Brändén 2011) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[Branden2011](#ref-Branden2011)] constructed a real-zero polynomial for which no positive integral power has a definite determinantal representation. This does not exclude another polynomial with the same positivity region. Indeed, Kummer (Kummer 2016) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[Kummer2016](#ref-Kummer2016)] proved that the related specialized Vámos polynomial has a spectrahedral hyperbolicity cone. Nor does failure of a specified sums-of-squares relaxation, such as those studied by Kummer, Plaumann, and Vinzant (Kummer, Plaumann, and Vinzant 2015) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[KummerPlaumannVinzant2015](#ref-KummerPlaumannVinzant2015)], exclude all finite lifts.

Positive representation results cover substantial classes. Brändén (Brändén 2014) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[Branden2014](#ref-Branden2014)] proved spectrahedrality for elementary symmetric hyperbolicity cones. Building on the representability criteria of Helton and Nie (Helton and Nie 2010) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[HeltonNie2010](#ref-HeltonNie2010)], Netzer and Sanyal (Netzer and Sanyal 2015, Theorem 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[NetzerSanyal2015](#ref-NetzerSanyal2015)] proved that a hyperbolicity cone is a spectrahedral shadow when each nonzero boundary point is a smooth point of its defining hyperbolic polynomial. Scheiderer (Scheiderer 2025) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[Scheiderer2025](#ref-Scheiderer2025)] subsequently obtained second-order-cone lifts for hyperbolicity cones with Nash-smooth boundary. Recent preprints prove spectrahedrality for five-variable hyperbolic cubics (Netzer 2026) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[Netzer2026](#ref-Netzer2026)] and for strictly hyperbolic polynomials (Kummer and Netzer 2026) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[KummerNetzer2026](#ref-KummerNetzer2026)]. These results concern special families or impose regularity hypotheses; they do not yield an exact finite lift for every hyperbolicity cone.

For general convex semialgebraic sets, Scheiderer (Scheiderer 2018) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[Scheiderer2018](#ref-Scheiderer2018)] established nonexistence of semidefinite lifts by local positivity obstructions. His finite-dimensional sum-of-squares pullback criterion and local arguments explain how a putative lift constrains functions near a smooth point. He explicitly asked whether these methods could settle the hyperbolicity-cone question (Scheiderer 2018, sec. 5.4) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[Scheiderer2018](#ref-Scheiderer2018)]. The argument here is closely related to that approach, but tests analytic Gram identities directly by a finite positive moment functional. Moment and sum-of-squares semidefinite methods are classical (Lasserre 2001) <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[Lasserre2001](#ref-Lasserre2001)]; our functional need not be integration against a measure. The general relationship between cone lifts and positive factorizations is developed by Gouveia, Parrilo, and Thomas (Gouveia, Parrilo, and Thomas 2013) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[GouveiaParriloThomas2013](#ref-GouveiaParriloThomas2013)]. We do not need to invoke their factorization theorem.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### The construction and proof strategy

We begin with a matrix-valued homogeneous quadratic map $Q:\mathbb R^m\to\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}_r$ that is positive semidefinite at every real input. Here $\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}_r$ denotes the real symmetric $r$-by-$r$ matrices. Section <a href="#sec:cone" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:cone">2</a> associates to $Q$ a homogeneous polynomial $p_Q$ and proves its hyperbolicity by a symmetric determinant identity on each line in the input space. It also identifies an exact affine section and projection of its closed cone: $$E_Q=\{(A,t,y): A\succeq0,\ \mathop{\mathrm{ran}}Q(y)\subseteq\mathop{\mathrm{ran}}A,
                    \ t\ge\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(A^\dagger Q(y))\}.$$ The symbol $A^\dagger$ denotes the Moore–Penrose inverse. It is enough to choose $Q$ so that $E_Q$ has no finite lift.

The difficulty is to force such an obstruction while keeping $Q(y)$ positive semidefinite for *every* $y$. Sections <a href="#sec:hankel" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:hankel">3</a> and <a href="#sec:separation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:separation">4</a> address this constraint. Let $H$ be the 36-dimensional space of quadratic forms in eight variables. Each linear functional $y$ on quartic forms determines a Hankel matrix $Y(y)$ on $H$ by $Y(y)(f,g)=y(fg)$. We construct a rank-twenty positive semidefinite Hankel matrix whose kernel has strong multiplication properties. After normalizing translations and dilations, its geometry yields a twenty-dimensional subspace $W\subset H$ and a finite set of projective classes of nonzero symmetric forms on $W$. The class of every nonzero limit of inverses of compressions to $W$ arising in the separation argument belongs to this set. The seed matrix is established by the finite-field certificate in Appendix <a href="#app:certificate" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:certificate">6</a>; the passage from its rank computations to a real positive semidefinite matrix is part of the proof.

For a sufficiently large integer $N$, finiteness lets us choose a tuple $w=(w_1,\ldots,w_N)\in W^N$ with $\sum_i w_i^TCw_i\ne0$ for every representative $C$ of one of these classes. We then choose $U\subset w^\perp\subset H^N$ so that convergence to $w$ of vectors $(I_N\otimes Y(y))u$, $u\in U$, would force one of these pairings to vanish. Thus $w$ lies outside the closure of those Hankel images. A symmetric form $g$ can be chosen nonnegative on every such image and negative at $w$. Using a basis matrix also denoted by $U$, we set $$G(y)=I_N\otimes Y(y),\qquad Q(y)=U^\top G(y)gG(y)U.$$ Nonnegativity on the Hankel images proves that $Q$ is admissible. The construction uses $m=330$ and $r=20N-1$, where one may take $N>10\cdot36^2$.

Section <a href="#sec:obstruction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:obstruction">5</a> turns the negative direction of $g$ into a local obstruction. Evaluation functionals at points $(1,s)$, $s\in\mathbb R^7$, give a degree-four polynomial patch $x(s)$ in $E_Q$. A positive definite moment functional $\Lambda$, applied to $x(c+\varepsilon s)$, produces points violating the trace inequality of $E_Q$ by a negative term of order $\varepsilon^4$. At the same time their $A$ coordinates are bounded below by a positive multiple of $\varepsilon^4 I$.

If a finite lift existed, semialgebraic selection would provide analytic lift coordinates and analytic Gram factors on an open part of this patch. Applying $\Lambda$ to their Taylor polynomials almost preserves the lift inequality. A mixing argument on the common support of the feasible pencil values repairs the small error without a strict feasibility assumption. It yields feasible points within $O(\varepsilon^{16})$ of the tested patch, too close to remove its order-four violation. The finite positive extension and finite-jet approximation lemmas isolate reusable parts of this obstruction. Together with the finite inverse-limit construction, they supply the proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.

<a id="sec:cone"></a>
### A determinant construction and its affine section

The first step is to place the obstruction in a tractable affine section. We prove hyperbolicity for every admissible quadratic matrix and identify the section exactly, including its singular boundary.

<a id="eq:pQ"></a>
Let $Q:\mathbb R^m\to\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}_r$ have homogeneous quadratic entries and satisfy $Q(y)\succeq0$ for every $y$. Put $n=r+1$. For $W=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&v^\top\\v&T\end{smallmatrix}\right)
\in\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}_n$, define <a id="eq:Phi"></a>$$\label{eq:Phi}
 \Phi_y(W)=
 \begin{pmatrix}
  \mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(Q(y)T)&-v^\top Q(y)\\
  -Q(y)v&aQ(y)
 \end{pmatrix}.$$ The polynomial used throughout the paper is $$\label{eq:pQ}
 p_Q(X,Z,y)=
 \det\bigl((\det X)Z-\Phi_y(\operatorname{adj}X)\bigr),
 \qquad X,Z\in\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}_n,\quad y\in\mathbb R^m.$$ The adjugate in this formula is the classical adjugate, so the formula is polynomial even when $X$ is singular.

<a id="stmt-prop-hyperbolic"></a>
<div id="prop:hyperbolic" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:block-cone"></a>
**Proposition 2**. *The polynomial $p_Q$ is homogeneous of degree $n(n+1)$ and hyperbolic with respect to $e=(I_n,I_n,0)$, with $p_Q(e)=1$. Fix $y$ and choose vectors $b_1,\ldots,b_n\in\mathbb R^r$ with $Q(y)=\sum_{j=1}^n b_jb_j^\top$. Define $$B_j=\begin{pmatrix}0&b_j^\top\\-b_j&0\end{pmatrix},
 \qquad
 \mathcal B=\begin{pmatrix}B_1\\\vdots\\B_n\end{pmatrix}.$$ Then the closed hyperbolicity cone $K_Q$ of $p_Q$ is characterized, at this value of $y$, by $$\label{eq:block-cone}
 (X,Z,y)\in K_Q
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \begin{pmatrix}I_n\otimes X&\mathcal B\\
                 \mathcal B^\top&Z\end{pmatrix}\succeq0.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-hyperbolic"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-hyperbolic</summary>

<a id="eq:line-determinant"></a>
*Proof.* The entries of the matrix in <a href="#eq:pQ" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pQ">[eq:pQ]</a> have degree $n+1$, and $p_Q(e)=1$, which proves the degree assertion. A Gram factorization as in the statement exists since $n>r$; zero vectors may be added. Direct multiplication gives $$\Phi_y(W)=\sum_{j=1}^n B_j^\top W B_j.$$ For every real $\eta$ we have the polynomial identity $$\label{eq:line-determinant}
 p_Q(X,Z,\eta y)=
 \det\begin{pmatrix}I_n\otimes X&\eta\mathcal B\\
                     \eta\mathcal B^\top&Z\end{pmatrix}.$$ Indeed, when $X$ is invertible the right side is $$(\det X)^n
 \det\bigl(Z-\eta^2\Phi_y(X^{-1})\bigr),$$ which equals the left side by homogeneity of $Q$ and the adjugate identity. Equality everywhere follows because both sides are polynomials in $X,Z,\eta$.

Write $D_y(X,Z)$ for the symmetric matrix on the right of <a href="#eq:block-cone" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:block-cone">[eq:block-cone]</a>. Substituting $tI_n-X,tI_n-Z,-y$ in <a href="#eq:line-determinant" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:line-determinant">[eq:line-determinant]</a> gives $$p_Q(te-(X,Z,y))=\det\bigl(tI_{n(n+1)}-D_y(X,Z)\bigr).$$ Its roots are real, being the eigenvalues of a real symmetric matrix. They are all nonnegative exactly when $D_y(X,Z)\succeq0$, proving both hyperbolicity and <a href="#eq:block-cone" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:block-cone">[eq:block-cone]</a>. ◻

</details>

The Gram factorization in Proposition <a href="#prop:hyperbolic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:hyperbolic">2</a> is chosen separately for each $y$. In particular, <a href="#eq:block-cone" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:block-cone">[eq:block-cone]</a> is not asserted to be a linear matrix inequality jointly in $X,Z,y$. The closed cone $K_Q$ is convex by Gårding’s theorem (Gårding 1959) <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[Garding1959](#ref-Garding1959)].

<a id="eq:EQ"></a>
For a positive semidefinite matrix $A$, let $A^\dagger$ denote its Moore–Penrose inverse. Define $$\label{eq:EQ}
 E_Q=\left\{(A,t,y):
 \begin{array}{l}
 A\in\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}_r,\ A\succeq0,\quad \mathop{\mathrm{ran}}Q(y)\subseteq\mathop{\mathrm{ran}}A,\\[2pt]
 t\geq\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\bigl(A^\dagger Q(y)\bigr)
 \end{array}\right\}.$$

<a id="stmt-prop-epigraph"></a>
<div id="prop:epigraph" class="proposition">

**Proposition 3**. *The set $E_Q$ is the image of the affine section $K_Q\cap\{X=\mathop{\mathrm{diag}}(1,A):A\in\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}_r\}$ under the projection $(X,Z,y)\mapsto(A,Z_{00},y)$. Consequently, if $K_Q$ is a spectrahedral shadow, then so is $E_Q$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-epigraph"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-epigraph</summary>

*Proof.* For a symmetric block matrix $\left(\begin{smallmatrix}H&C\\C^\top&Z\end{smallmatrix}\right)$, positive semidefiniteness is equivalent to $$H\succeq0,\qquad \mathop{\mathrm{ran}}C\subseteq\mathop{\mathrm{ran}}H,\qquad
 Z-C^\top H^\dagger C\succeq0.$$ To see the boundary assertion directly, positivity forces every vector in $\ker H$ to annihilate $C$, by testing the quadratic form on $(u,\lambda v)$. The asserted range condition then follows, and a triangular congruence reduces the matrix to the direct sum of $H$ and $Z-C^\top H^\dagger C$.

Apply this criterion to <a href="#eq:block-cone" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:block-cone">[eq:block-cone]</a> with $X=\mathop{\mathrm{diag}}(1,A)$. Its range condition says $b_j\in\mathop{\mathrm{ran}}A$ for every $j$, equivalently $\mathop{\mathrm{ran}}Q(y)\subseteq\mathop{\mathrm{ran}}A$. Moreover, $$\mathcal B^\top(I_n\otimes X)^\dagger\mathcal B
 =\Phi_y(\mathop{\mathrm{diag}}(1,A^\dagger))
 =\mathop{\mathrm{diag}}\bigl(\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(A^\dagger Q(y)),Q(y)\bigr).$$ It follows that every point of the affine section projects into $E_Q$. Conversely, given $(A,t,y)\in E_Q$, take $Z=\mathop{\mathrm{diag}}(t,Q(y))$ to obtain a point of that section. This proves the asserted equality with no closure operation. Affine sections and coordinate projections preserve the existence of an affine semidefinite lift, proving the final assertion. ◻

</details>

We will construct $Q$ for which $E_Q$ has no such lift. The rest of the argument therefore concerns <a href="#eq:EQ" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:EQ">[eq:EQ]</a>, not the much larger determinant in <a href="#eq:pQ" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pQ">[eq:pQ]</a>.

<a id="sec:hankel"></a>
### Hankel matrices and finitely many inverse limits

We construct a positive semidefinite Hankel matrix of rank $20$ and a $20$-dimensional subspace $W$ with a finiteness property: certain nonzero limits of inverses of compressions to $W$ have only finitely many projective directions. The construction has two parts. A rank calculation, proved in Appendix <a href="#app:certificate" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:certificate">6</a>, supplies a point at which a kernel-projection map has sufficiently large derivative. We then divide out translations and dilations, choose a finite fiber of the resulting map, and use that fiber to classify the inverse limits.

<a id="pm-h-7"></a>
#### The rank stratum and the seed matrix

Let $x=(x_0,x_1,\ldots,x_7)$ and put $$H=\mathbb R[x]_2=L\oplus P,
 \qquad
 L=\langle x_0^2,x_0x_1,\ldots,x_0x_7\rangle,
 \qquad
 P=\mathbb R[x_1,\ldots,x_7]_2.$$ Subscripts denote homogeneous degree. Monomials are our coordinate bases, and these bases are orthonormal whenever we take an orthogonal complement or compression. Setting $x_0=1$ identifies $H$ with polynomials of degree at most two in $s=(s_1,\ldots,s_7)$. In this identification, $L$ consists of the constant and linear terms and $P$ consists of the quadratic terms. Write <a id="eq:hankel-dimensions"></a>$$\label{eq:hankel-dimensions}
 h=\dim H=36,\qquad d_*=20,\qquad k=h-d_*=16,
 \qquad q=d_*-\dim L=12.$$

<a id="eq:hankel-functional"></a>
Let $\mathcal H\subset\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}(H)$ be the space of Hankel matrices $$\label{eq:hankel-functional}
 Y(p,p')=y(pp'),\qquad p,p'\in H,
 \qquad y\in\mathbb R[x]_4^*.$$ Multiplication $\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}^2H\to\mathbb R[x]_4$ is onto, so $y\mapsto Y$ is injective and $\dim\mathcal H=\binom{11}{4}=330$. We also call $Y$ a moment matrix. This terminology refers only to <a href="#eq:hankel-functional" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hankel-functional">[eq:hankel-functional]</a>; no representing measure is assumed. In the chart $x_0=1$, the functional $y$ acts on polynomials in $s$ of degree at most four.

For subspaces of polynomial spaces, a product such as $JH_0$ denotes the linear span of all products of one element of each space. We use the following algebraic input.

<a id="stmt-lem-seed"></a>
<div id="lem:seed" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:pure-cubic-spanning"></a>
**Lemma 4** (Seed matrix). *There is a positive semidefinite matrix $T\in\mathcal H$ of rank $d_*=20$ with the following properties. Its restriction to $L$ is positive definite. If $$J=\ker T,\qquad K=\operatorname{proj}_P J,
 \qquad H_0=\{p\in H:T(p,L)=0\},$$ then $\dim K=k=16$, multiplication $$\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}^2K\longrightarrow\mathbb R[x_1,\ldots,x_7]_4$$ is injective, and $$\label{eq:pure-cubic-spanning}
 K\langle x_1,\ldots,x_7\rangle
   =\mathbb R[x_1,\ldots,x_7]_3.$$ Finally, $JH_0$ has codimension $9$ in $\mathbb R[x]_4$.*

</div>

The proof in Appendix <a href="#app:certificate" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:certificate">6</a> begins with two cyclic orbits of real points and deforms their moment matrix inside the rank-$20$ stratum. All rank conditions needed for that deformation are verified there by explicit minors.

Let $\mathcal X$ be the set of projective classes $[Y]\in\mathbf P(\mathcal H)$ such that $\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}Y=d_*$, $\ker Y\cap L=0$, and the projected kernel $K_Y=\operatorname{proj}_P(\ker Y)$ satisfies the two multiplication conditions of Lemma <a href="#lem:seed" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:seed">4</a>. Positivity is not part of the definition of $\mathcal X$. Define $$\pi:\mathcal X\longrightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{Gr}}(k,P),
 \qquad \pi([Y])=K_Y.$$

<a id="stmt-lem-hankel-stratum"></a>
<div id="lem:hankel-stratum" class="lemma">

**Lemma 5**. *The set $\mathcal X$ is a smooth semialgebraic manifold of dimension $$D=330-1-\frac{k(k+1)}2=193.$$ Before projectivization, the tangent space at a representative $Y$ is the space of $Y'\in\mathcal H$ whose restriction to $\ker Y\times\ker Y$ is zero.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-hankel-stratum"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-hankel-stratum</summary>

*Proof.* Put $J_Y=\ker Y$. Taking highest homogeneous parts in the affine chart identifies $J_Y$ with $K_Y$. Injectivity of multiplication on $\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}^2K_Y$ therefore implies injectivity on $\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}^2J_Y$: a relation among products in $J_Y$ would have a relation among their highest parts. By duality, restriction of Hankel forms gives a surjection $$\mathcal H\longrightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}(J_Y).$$ Choose a complement of $J_Y$ in $H$. The compression of $Y$ to this complement is invertible: a vector in the complement orthogonal for $Y$ to that complement is orthogonal for $Y$ to all of $H$, and hence lies in $J_Y$. In a neighborhood of $Y$, rank $d_*$ is thus equivalent to the vanishing of a $k$-by-$k$ symmetric Schur complement. At $Y$, the derivative of this Schur complement is restriction to $J_Y$. The implicit function theorem gives a smooth manifold of codimension $k(k+1)/2=136$ in $\mathcal H$, with the asserted tangent space. The condition $\ker Y\cap L=0$ and the multiplication conditions on $K_Y$ are open rank conditions. Finally, projectivization removes the nonzero scalar parameter, giving dimension $193$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
#### Removing translations and dilations

<a id="eq:kernel-graph"></a>
The fibers of $\pi$ contain the orbits of affine translations and positive dilations in the $s$ variables. We give explicit coordinates for removing these eight parameters. Because $J_Y\cap L=0$, its elements, in the affine chart, have a unique description $$\label{eq:kernel-graph}
 J_Y=\{p(s)+b(p)(s)+a(p):p\in K_Y\},$$ where $b:K_Y\to\mathbb R[s]_1$ takes values in homogeneous linear forms and $a:K_Y\to\mathbb R$ is linear. These coefficient maps depend continuously and semialgebraically on $[Y]$.

<a id="eq:translation-kernel-coefficients"></a>
<a id="eq:dilation-kernel-coefficients"></a>
Pushforward of moments by $s\mapsto s+t$ transforms a kernel polynomial by $f(s)\mapsto f(s-t)$. Thus translation changes the coefficient maps by $$\label{eq:translation-kernel-coefficients}
 b_t(p)=b(p)-\partial_t p,
 \qquad
 a_t(p)=a(p)-b(p)(t)+p(t),$$ where $\partial_t p$ is the directional derivative of $p$ in direction $t$. Pushforward by $s\mapsto\lambda s$, with $\lambda>0$, gives $$\label{eq:dilation-kernel-coefficients}
 b\longmapsto\lambda b,
 \qquad a\longmapsto\lambda^2 a.$$ Both actions are congruences on moment matrices. They preserve Hankel structure, rank, and $\pi$, and preserve positive semidefiniteness when it is present.

<a id="stmt-lem-kernel-affine-action"></a>
<div id="lem:kernel-affine-action" class="lemma">

**Lemma 6**. *For every $[Y]\in\mathcal X$, the map $$\Delta_{K_Y}:\mathbb R^7\longrightarrow
   \operatorname{Hom}(K_Y,\mathbb R[s]_1),
 \qquad t\longmapsto(p\mapsto\partial_t p)$$ is injective, and the coefficient maps $a,b$ in <a href="#eq:kernel-graph" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:kernel-graph">[eq:kernel-graph]</a> are not both zero. The seven infinitesimal translations and the infinitesimal dilation give eight independent variations of the kernel graph. These assertions, including the independence of the variations, also hold over $\mathbf C$ under the same rank and multiplication hypotheses.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-kernel-affine-action"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-kernel-affine-action</summary>

*Proof.* If $t\ne0$ belongs to the kernel of $\Delta_{K_Y}$, a linear change of the pure variables makes $t$ the first coordinate direction. All quadratics in $K_Y$ are then independent of that coordinate, so their products with linear forms cannot span its cube. This contradicts <a href="#eq:pure-cubic-spanning" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pure-cubic-spanning">[eq:pure-cubic-spanning]</a>.

Suppose next that $a=b=0$, so $J_Y=K_Y$ in the chart. The kernel condition and <a href="#eq:pure-cubic-spanning" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pure-cubic-spanning">[eq:pure-cubic-spanning]</a> force all moments of degree three to vanish. Multiplying that spanning identity by linear forms shows that $K_YP=\mathbb R[s]_4$, so all moments of degree four vanish as well. With coordinates ordered by degrees $0,1,2$, the moment matrix consequently has the form $$\begin{pmatrix}
  *&*&*\\ *&*&0\\ *&0&0
 \end{pmatrix},$$ where the degree-zero block has size one and the degree-one block has size seven. The last block row has rank at most one, and the first eight rows have rank at most eight. Thus $\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}Y\le9$, a contradiction.

For independence of the infinitesimal variations, suppose a translation in direction $t$ and a dilation with infinitesimal coefficient $\alpha$ have zero combined variation of the graph. Equations <a href="#eq:translation-kernel-coefficients" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:translation-kernel-coefficients">[eq:translation-kernel-coefficients]</a> and <a href="#eq:dilation-kernel-coefficients" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dilation-kernel-coefficients">[eq:dilation-kernel-coefficients]</a> give $$\alpha b(p)=\partial_t p,
 \qquad 2\alpha a(p)=b(p)(t)
 \quad(p\in K_Y).$$ If $\alpha=0$, injectivity of $\Delta_{K_Y}$ gives $t=0$. If $\alpha\ne0$, put $c=t/\alpha$. Then $b(p)=\partial_c p$ and $a(p)=p(c)$, so every polynomial in the graph is $p(s+c)$. A translation would therefore produce the already excluded case $a=b=0$. This proves independence. Each argument is algebraic and applies equally over $\mathbf C$. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:kernel-normalization"></a>
Equip the spaces of coefficient maps with the Euclidean inner products induced by the monomial bases. We normalize $[Y]$ in two steps. First choose the unique translation for which $b\perp\mathop{\mathrm{ran}}\Delta_{K_Y}$. Explicitly, its parameter is $$t=(\Delta_{K_Y}^*\Delta_{K_Y})^{-1}\Delta_{K_Y}^*b.$$ Next choose the unique positive dilation for which $$\label{eq:kernel-normalization}
 \|b\|^2+\|a\|^2=1.$$ For the translated coefficients, this amounts to solving $\lambda^2\|b\|^2+\lambda^4\|a\|^2=1$. The left side is strictly increasing from zero to infinity, by Lemma <a href="#lem:kernel-affine-action" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:kernel-affine-action">6</a>.

<a id="stmt-lem-hankel-normalization"></a>
<div id="lem:hankel-normalization" class="lemma">

**Lemma 7**. *The normalized matrices, together with their data $(K_Y,b,a)$, form a semialgebraic set $\mathcal M$ of dimension $185$. Normalization is continuous, and each $[Y]\in\mathcal X$ is uniquely recovered from its normalized data and its seven translation parameters and positive dilation parameter. Moreover, $\mathcal M$ has a compact ambient semialgebraic space in which the projection to $\mathop{\mathrm{Gr}}(k,P)$ is continuous.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-hankel-normalization"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-hankel-normalization</summary>

*Proof.* The translation formula is continuous because $\Delta_{K_Y}$ has constant full column rank. The unique positive solution for $\lambda$ is continuous and semialgebraic as well. Dilation preserves the orthogonality condition on $b$, so the two normalizations are compatible. Conversely, start with normalized data, undo its positive dilation, and then undo its translation. Applying the two normalization steps recovers the prescribed parameters. This gives a semialgebraic homeomorphism between $\mathcal X$ and $\mathcal M\times\mathbb R^7\times\mathbb R_{>0}$, and hence $\dim\mathcal M=193-8=185$.

To specify a compact ambient space, retain the projective matrix $[Y]$ and the subspace $K_Y$, and extend $a,b$ by zero on $K_Y^\perp$ in $P$. The extended maps have the same norms as the original maps. Condition <a href="#eq:kernel-normalization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:kernel-normalization">[eq:kernel-normalization]</a> places their pair on a fixed unit sphere. The product of this sphere with $\mathbf P(\mathcal H)$ and $\mathop{\mathrm{Gr}}(k,P)$ is compact, and projection to the last factor is continuous. We take the closure of $\mathcal M$ in this product. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
#### Selecting a finite fiber

We now show that the positive semidefinite part of $\mathcal X$ has enough distinct projected kernels to avoid both infinite normalized fibers and limits at the boundary of $\mathcal M$. We use the standard dimension and fiber-dimension theorems for semialgebraic sets, as well as the fact that the frontier $\overline S\setminus S$ of a semialgebraic set has smaller dimension than $S$; see (Bochnak, Coste, and Roy 1998) <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[BCR1998](#ref-BCR1998)]. A zero-dimensional semialgebraic set is finite.

<a id="eq:projection-tangent"></a>
At any $T$ representing a point of $\mathcal X$, put $J=\ker T$ and $H_0=\{p:T(p,L)=0\}$. The kernel of the derivative of the kernel-projection map, before projectivizing, is $$\label{eq:projection-tangent}
 \{Y'\in\mathcal H:Y'(J)\subset T(L)\}
       =(JH_0)^\perp.$$ Here the right side is an annihilator in $\mathbb R[x]_4^*$, identified with $\mathcal H$. To verify the identity, continue a vector $j\in J$ along a differentiable curve of kernels. Its first variation $j'$ must satisfy $$Y'j+Tj'=0.$$ The projected kernel has zero first variation precisely when the pure part of each $j'$ lies in $K$. Subtracting an element of $J$ then makes $j'$ belong to $L$, without changing this equation. Thus the condition is $Y'J\subset T(L)$. Conversely, that condition allows all the corrections to be chosen in $L$; it also implies $Y'|_{J\times J}=0$, so Lemma <a href="#lem:hankel-stratum" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:hankel-stratum">5</a> gives an actual rank-stratum tangent. Finally, $H_0=T(L)^\perp$, which proves the annihilator description.

At the seed matrix of Lemma <a href="#lem:seed" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:seed">4</a>, the space in <a href="#eq:projection-tangent" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:projection-tangent">[eq:projection-tangent]</a> has dimension $9$. The rank stratum before projectivizing has dimension $194$, so the derivative of $\pi$ has rank $194-9=185$. Projectivization removes one kernel direction, namely scaling, and leaves this derivative rank unchanged. Near the seed matrix, rank remains $20$ and its nonzero eigenvalues remain positive. Its $L$ block remains positive definite as well. Consequently the $\pi$-image of matrices with these positivity properties has dimension at least $185$. It has dimension at most $185$ because $\pi$ factors through $\mathcal M$.

The seed’s final condition can also be retained in this neighborhood. Indeed, an invertible $321$-row product minor for $JH_0$ remains invertible nearby. On the other hand, scaling, translations, and dilation always give nine independent tangent directions in <a href="#eq:projection-tangent" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:projection-tangent">[eq:projection-tangent]</a>: the eight affine directions have independent kernel variations by Lemma <a href="#lem:kernel-affine-action" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:kernel-affine-action">6</a>, whereas scaling has zero kernel variation. Thus $\dim(JH_0)\le321$ throughout this neighborhood.

<a id="stmt-prop-finite-normalized-fiber"></a>
<div id="prop:finite-normalized-fiber" class="proposition">

**Proposition 8**. *The matrix $T$ in Lemma <a href="#lem:seed" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:seed">4</a> may be chosen so that, for $K=\operatorname{proj}_P\ker T$,*

1.  *the fiber $\mathcal M_K$ of $\mathcal M\to\mathop{\mathrm{Gr}}(k,P)$ is finite;*

2.  *no point of $\overline{\mathcal M}\setminus\mathcal M$ projects to $K$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-finite-normalized-fiber"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-finite-normalized-fiber</summary>

*Proof.* The set of subspaces over which the fiber of $\mathcal M$ has positive dimension has dimension at most $184$, by the fiber-dimension theorem and $\dim\mathcal M=185$. The frontier of $\mathcal M$ has dimension at most $184$, so its projection to $\mathop{\mathrm{Gr}}(k,P)$ has dimension at most $184$ as well. The positive semidefinite image just constructed has dimension $185$. We can therefore choose $K$ in that image outside both exceptional sets, and choose a corresponding positive semidefinite $T$ in the neighborhood under consideration. Its fiber is nonempty and zero-dimensional, hence finite. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:fixed-complements"></a>
Fix this $T$ and $K$ for the rest of the paper, and set $$\label{eq:fixed-complements}
 R=K^\perp\subset P,\qquad W=L\oplus R.$$ Thus $H=W\oplus K$ is an orthogonal decomposition, $\dim R=q=12$, and $\dim W=d_*=20$.

<a id="eq:finite-inverse-directions"></a>
Each normalized class $[\bar Y]\in\mathcal M_K$ determines a nonzero symmetric matrix on $W$, up to scale. Let $\bar A$ be the compression of a representative $\bar Y$ to $W$. The kernel of $\bar Y$ is a graph over $K$ with lower terms in $L$, so $W$ is a complement of that kernel. The same nondegeneracy argument used in Lemma <a href="#lem:hankel-stratum" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:hankel-stratum">5</a> shows that $\bar A$ is invertible, even if $\bar Y$ is indefinite. Define $C_{\bar Y}\in\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}(W)$ to have block form $$\label{eq:finite-inverse-directions}
 C_{\bar Y}=
 \begin{pmatrix}0&0\\0&(\bar A^{-1})_{R,R}\end{pmatrix}
 \quad\hbox{on }W=L\oplus R,
 \qquad
 \mathcal C=\{[C_{\bar Y}]:[\bar Y]\in\mathcal M_K\}.$$ If its $R,R$ block were zero, the invertible operator $\bar A^{-1}$ would map $R$ into $L$, which is impossible because $12>8$. Thus $\mathcal C$ is a finite nonempty set of projective classes of nonzero symmetric matrices on $W$.

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
#### The inverse-limit property

To connect the normalized fiber to arbitrary limiting sequences, we first need a rank correction that remains small even when some matrix entries diverge. All norms below are fixed Euclidean operator norms; convergence is equivalent in any choice of such norms.

<a id="stmt-lem-hankel-rank-correction"></a>
<div id="lem:hankel-rank-correction" class="lemma">

**Lemma 9** (Uniform rank correction). *Use the fixed decomposition $H=W\oplus K$ from <a href="#eq:fixed-complements" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:fixed-complements">[eq:fixed-complements]</a>. Suppose $Y_i\in\mathcal H$ have blocks $$Y_i=\begin{pmatrix}A_i&B_i\\B_i^T&D_i\end{pmatrix},$$ where $A_i$ is invertible, $\sup_i\|A_i^{-1}\|<\infty$, and $$A_i^{-1}B_i\longrightarrow0,
 \qquad D_i-B_i^TA_i^{-1}B_i\longrightarrow0.$$ There are Hankel perturbations $\Delta_i\to0$ such that $Y_i^\sharp=Y_i+\Delta_i$ has rank $d_*$. Its $W$ compression $A_i^\sharp$ satisfies $$(A_i^\sharp)^{-1}-A_i^{-1}\longrightarrow0,$$ and $\ker Y_i^\sharp\to K$, or equivalently $\mathop{\mathrm{ran}}Y_i^\sharp\to W$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-hankel-rank-correction"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-hankel-rank-correction</summary>

<a id="eq:schur-differential"></a>
*Proof.* On matrices whose $W$ block is invertible, write $\mathcal S(Y)=D-B^TA^{-1}B$ for the Schur complement on $K$. For a variation $\Delta$ with blocks $(a,b,d)$ and $F=A^{-1}B$, $$\label{eq:schur-differential}
 D\mathcal S_Y(\Delta)
   =d-b^TF-F^Tb+F^TaF.$$ At $Y_i$, these derivatives tend to the restriction map $\Delta\mapsto\Delta_{K,K}$ on Hankel space. That restriction map is onto $\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}(K)$ because multiplication on $\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}^2K$ is injective. Choose a fixed linear right inverse $\mathcal E:\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}(K)\to\mathcal H$.

We record why an implicit-function argument is uniform in $i$. Put $M=\sup_i\|A_i^{-1}\|$. On a fixed sufficiently small ball of perturbations, the inverse of the perturbed $W$ block obeys $$(A_i+a)^{-1}=(I+A_i^{-1}a)^{-1}A_i^{-1},
 \qquad \|(A_i+a)^{-1}\|\le2M.$$ Moreover, $$(A_i+a)^{-1}(B_i+b)
   =(I+A_i^{-1}a)^{-1}(A_i^{-1}B_i+A_i^{-1}b)$$ is uniformly bounded there. Differentiating the inverse and this last expression gives $$\delta(A^{-1})=-A^{-1}(\delta A)A^{-1},
 \qquad
 \delta F=A^{-1}(\delta B-(\delta A)F).$$ Together with <a href="#eq:schur-differential" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:schur-differential">[eq:schur-differential]</a>, these identities give a uniform bound for the second derivatives of $\mathcal S$ on that ball. This bound uses neither boundedness of $A_i$ nor boundedness of $B_i$.

Define $f_i(z)=\mathcal S(Y_i+\mathcal E z)$ for $z\in\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}(K)$ in a fixed small ball. We have $f_i(0)\to0$ and $Df_i(0)\to I$. Shrinking the fixed ball if needed, the uniform second-derivative bound gives $\|Df_i(z)-I\|\le1/2$ there for all sufficiently large $i$.
